OCCULT virtue of herbs, stones, metals, animals, worlds and actions are of God, who is the beginning and end of all things, who can suspend their action so that they shall wholly desist from fulfilling their regular functions, and will facilitate in a wonderful degree the study of OCCULT science. 

FASCINATION. 

That the art of fascination or binding has great virtues and wonderful efficacy there can be no doubt, for by the faculty of fascination, or more properly the magnetic or Occult binding, of a person into love, hatred, sickness or health, confound, subvert, consume, change or suspend the faculties of men; also the binding of dogs so that they cannot bark, the binding of birds and wild beasts so that they cannot run or fly away are possibilities which hardly seem credible, yet known to be true by experience.

Fascination is here spoken of as binding, because results are affected by a piercing look, glance, or observation, and many persons have become bewitched by looking directly into the eyes of those who bewitch or fascinate them, their eyes being reciprocally bent magnetically one upon the other; it is then that the spirit or magnetism overcomes and fascinates by becoming joined to the other, then strong ligations are made, and most violent love and passions are aroused.

So great a power is there in facination, that the enchanter with a sudden look, a darting glance which pierces into the innermost depth of the soul, renders the other subservient to his will and affections.

This subtle invisible magnetic influence which is generated by the firm, determined and ardent will of the soul, is projected through the physical eye to the person previously disposed to be fascinated or bewitched.

Therefore the gaze being intensely concentrated upon anyone, with a strong imagination, which is the vehicle of the spirit, infects the heart, mind or soul of an individual, controlling and holding spellbound; the same as the snake charms or fascinates the gentle birds.

There can be no mistake that the eyes are the instrument or medium of the enchanter who by an earnest intent of the mind, takes possession of the faculties and beguiles the reason and senses of the person he desires to influence.

The marvelous potency of Vital Magnetism is that subtle, intangible and strange power which lies among the invisible latent possibilities of every human soul. Very few have developed this Occult element. But once understood, there is revealed to the student the secret and mysterious law of this all potential force, by which one can exert an irresistible influence over people, who are entirely unconscious of the fact that you are throwing a hypnotic condition of fascination around them. Because they have no tangible evidence nor knowledge of the existence or possibilities of this subtle, invisible power, force, influence, spiritual or vital magnetism of the human mind or soul which is known to the Western student of Practical Psychology as Hypnotic influence, Per sonal or Animal Magnetism, Magnetic Personality, Mes meric influence, etc.

Every intelligent and observing person realizes that the human mind or will is capable of fascinating, influ-

MAGNETISM 

47 encing, affecting or impressing the mentality of man, woman or child, and that this subtle influence has been used in the past by those who understood the nature possibilities and law of application, to secure love, position and wealth, also the confidence and esteem of others.

Those who possess this ability have an inevitable advantage in both social and commercial life. And any person, who through ignorance, prejudice, or obstinacy will say that there is no validity or reason or reality in the philosophy of Vital Magnetism condemns a natural cause.

Therefore let those who would attain knowledge in the study of Occultism and become perfect in the magical virtue and efficacy of their own souls and latent possibilities, constantly meditate and intensely desire that the Omnipotent Creator, the First Cause and Archetype of all things, graciously and mercifully illuminate their souls; without which, they grope about in darkness and the pitiful uncertainty of materialism, ever sub. ject to the results and delusions thereof. 

PERSONAL MAGNETISM. 

There is constantly emanating from every person his own particular personal magnetism. It is this particular invisible element which enables the faithful dog to scent and trace his master through a crowded thoroughfare, the hunting dog to follow and run wild game to its lair. This same Magnetism enables the bloodhound to run the criminal to earth. Personal or animal Magnetism is the medium by which and through which, persons are either attracted to or repelled by each other, its na-

ture being the key of success in our dealings in this world. Success in worldly affairs does not always depend upon intelligence or education, as is demonstrated to all who have observed the success of the man or woman of a magnetic personality, but depends entirely upon the mental or psychic forces.

All persons, to a greater or lesser degree, possess these latent forces, but a few have developed them to their fullest extent. 

Take the Magnetism of a fascinating young lady, which is invested with a subtle, indefinable charm, so subtle, intangible, alluring, that the effects of its farreaching influence are felt by all with whom she comes in contact. Personal or Vital Magnetism radiates from the loving parent's heart, emanates from the powerful public speaker, as it does from the teacher whose strong Magnetic personality gives her discipline over her pupils. 

This invisible influence goes forth unseen, but ever felt in the gentle tone and kindly ministrations of the skilled nurse. This subtle influence is felt by the very presence of the trusted physician, whose magnetic look and reassuring words give to the poor, suffering and afflicted soul new energy, life and hope.

Magnetism breathes and pulsates in every word, look and action of the marvelous Patti enthralling and charming the soul by her magic spell. And so the masses are swayed, charmed and fascinated by this selfsame Magnetism or Hypnotic influence which is looked upon as a wonderful gift by Providence to a few favored ones. 

All of our great leaders in Congress sway and bind their audiences, either consciously or unconsciously by conforming to the law of supreme soul force. History

MAGNETISM 

49 records many illustrations of the absolute certainty and power of a magnetic personality.

Our late President, William McKinley, who was just, tender and wise in his kindly ministrations of the affairs of the nation, by his firm and diplomatic manner, so well known to his associates, exerted this influence, which radiated from his powerful personality, ever leading the people lovingly and safely; always winning their confidence, and retaining throughout his executive life, their undying affection and unquestioning, loyal obedience.

The gracious influence of his strong personality was always forcibly felt in the brilliant glance of his manly eye, scintillated in his welcome smile, glowed in his friendly word and thrilled those who felt the hearty and firm grasp of his hand.

In every sphere of life the cultivation and development of Magnetic qualities are of the highest importance, as this force is latent in every human soul, a sacred gift and priceless jewel, whose assiduous cultivation always bestows benefit.

This magical power belongs to the inward man or soul whose spiritual strength, faculty or efficacy is best stirred into action by intense concentration and earnest intention, combined with spoken words in which there is a wonderful Occult virtue whereby we take possession of the faculties and bind or attract those things which we desire or delight in.

Indeed the virtue of man's words are so great and wonderful that when pronounced with a fervent constancy of mind or soul they are able to subvert nature or bind the elements.

The three Evangelists relate how Jesus "Appeased

the troubled waters and roaring deep." Matt. 8:23-27.

Mark 4:35-41. Luke 8:22-25. 

The Saviour, who was asleep in the hinder part of the ship when there arose a great tempest on the sea, the waves beating into the ship so that it was now full, arose and rebuked the winds and the sea; and there was a great calm. Christ spoke first to his disciples, calming their fear and agitation with a word, His magical address of "Peace, be still;" to the furious elements which caused a great calm was a revelation. The men marveled, saying: "What manner of man is this, that even the winds and the sea obey him?"

Christ again turned to them and more deliberately rebuked their lack of faith in Him. "And He saith unto them, Why are ye fearful, O ye of little faith?” 

The Lord rebukes a fever (Luke 4:39), which rebuke was not unheard or unheeded: for not "willingly" was the fever or "condition" made subject to man's vanity." (Romans, 8:20.)

The Occult forces of nature which were constituted at first, as man's servant or hand-maid, rise up against him, and become the instruments of his hurt and harm, trouble and sorrow.

But even in the moment of their wildest uproar and devastation they recognize and yield to the voice of 

Him who is the rightful Master, and gladly return to their allegiance to man, and in this, to their proper place of service to the human race.

Man is not by nature a creature of circumstances but should always endeavor to be master of the event, ever reclaiming and reasserting his Psychic or Magnetic possibilities.

MAGNETISM 51 Here is a lesson of obvious utility and necessity, namely, that there is no force, Magnetism or convincing influence in any person's words, except they are pronounced with a fervent constancy of the mind or soul.

As an illustration of the import of words spoken vehemently and their natural virtue, let a person who cares to convince himself, learn that rape seeds, sown with intense cursing and imprecations, grow the fairer, and thrive; but if with praises and kind words, the reverse.

Experience will teach the student of vital Magnetism and Occult Philosophy the truthfulness of the following statement that-All Charms, Amulets, Numbers, Divine Names, Mystic Characters, Symbolical Engrav ings, Magical Ceremonies, Idols, Seals of Spirits, Invocations, Prayers, Human Imprecations, all Talismanic

Operations, the many different forms of the Exorcisms, Benedictions and Conjurations, as well the Consecra tions and Benediction of Water (Holy Water), Fire, Oil, Candles, Wax, Burial Ground, Localities, and all of the instruments and materials used by Priests, Ministers, Philosophers, Necromancers, Conjurers, etc., are impotent without words spoken with fervent constancy of the heart and soul together with an intense concentration of the mind and will, because words are formulated thoughts, the speech of the speaker, and the image of the thing desired, signified or spoken of. Therefore, whatever wonderful effects are intended or desired, let the same be performed with the addition of intensely spoken words significant of the will or desire of the speaker. For spoken words or verbal suggestions are a vehicle which carries an OCCULT virtue or in other words the Magnetism of the speaker, which is projected

out of the body into space by the spiritual or magical efficacy of the soul; consequently this forcible power of the spirit should be breathed out with vehemence, and an arduous and intense desire.

The OCCULT virtue of words spoken in this intensity is carried forward to the hearer at a vast distance, no other body intervening, and is known in the western countries as telepathy, clairaudience, etc., etc. As the result of the spiritual action of the soul or mind, while the body is dormant, the physical sense, being for the time suspended, words are oftentimes delivered to us, seemingly by others, while we are asleep, therefore, negative and in a condition to easily receive a mental impression, whereby we seem to talk and converse; these phenomena proceed from spiritual and OCCULT causes and have often manifested strange and singular things to us while we are asleep. And upon waking we have thought naught of, until conviction of the truth of the existence of the magnetic virtue of the soul, taught us credulity.

These Phenomena are not alike manifested to everyone,-only to those who have seriously attended to the contemplation of the abstruse OCCULT causes of nature. 

There are many who will not agree with the writer, merely because they themselves are too materialistic to comprehend such things.

Therefore, let it be known that no vocal conversations, verbal or mental suggestions are of any effect whatsoever, except they be spoken or made with a certain intensity, while the mind and will is intensely concentrated upon the person or persons addressed.

In conclusion the writer will state that history, obser. vation and personal experience are all that can teach us that the Psychic or Occult forces of the human soul, which are invisible, will produce these effects, and other 

Occult phenomena.

CHAPTER III. 

INDUCTION OF HYPNOSIS. 

Hypnosis a Psychological Condition-Methods of Inducing Hypnosis-Mesmere's System-Braid's Passes-Berger's Fascination-Lasague's-Faria's-Magnets-Drugs-Luys' Mirror Rotary-Bernheim's Method-The Writer's Method-Is of Waking a Subject-Different Stages of Hypnosis — Gurney Forel-Tuckey - Liebeault-Bernheim-De-hypnotization- Dangerous Points-Duration of Hypnotic Sleep-Susceptible Subjects-Charcot's School-Different Schools-Modern Mesmerism and the Paris School-Drowsy Influence—Hypnotic 

Condition Light Somnambulism - Deep Somnambulism - 

Classifications-Hypnosis-Waking a Subject - Duration of 

Sleep-Hysteria-The Cataleptic State-Fascinated- Occult 

Philosophy-Somnambulism-De Laurence's Method-Delusion. 

It is clear that the terms signified by the names of "Mesmerism," "Personal Magnetism," "Animal Magnetism, "Electro-biology," and other terms were incapable of any serious study by the student of Occult Philosophy or Practical Psychology, since they concerned a state which, from a Psychological standpoint was altogether incomprehensible, if not thought to be supernatural; whether true or false, they may be said to stand much in the same position towards the scientific world as spiritualism and theosophy. By Psychologists it is known that this state of Hypnosis, whether it is called hypnotic, Mesmeric, or any other term, is one of a purely Psycho-

logical condition, and this theory has been adhered to by every scientific student who has studied the Occult in ages that have past. Of course there have been in the past many interesting questions, and phenomena such as telepathy, suspended animation, clairaudience, thought transference, and clairvoyance, upon which it would demand unlimited space to give specific instructions; and it must be stated that the Hypnotic condition per se is absolutely necessary to produce this phenomena, whether said Hypnotic state is induced by an operator or is the result of auto-suggestion of self-induction. 

Telepathy, as has been demonstrated in ages that are past, is possible between two persons, and it is by no means unlikely that this same mental sympathy which exists between man and man while in the normal state is wonderfully increased during the Hypnotic sleep. 

The writer and those who have witnessed his demonstrations realize that experiments of this sort are entirely conclusive and satisfactory. Of course all these strange Psychological conditions present problems of great interest to the student; and if he does not attempt to seek verification it will be because he is not seriously interested in things Psychological.

Many methods and systems have been taught and used for the induction of Hypnosis; regarding the best methods for inducing the Hypnotic sleep; details of the same will be given later on in this work. The success of many of the so-called methods depended not so much on the alleged cause, but on conditions which, though always necessarily present, were not regarded by the operators as having any thing essential to do with the process of Hypnotizing, therefore, it will be well to give

a summary of the various methods and systems which have been employed and practiced in the past, both in this country and Europe.

MESMER'S METHODS. 

Mesmer did not rely on any one method or system, but made use of numerous contrivances, by means of which he obtained an influence over his subjects and patients. 

The essential point was that the patient should be in direct contact with the operator, magnetizer, or the magnetized baquet (so called) in order that the "magnetic fluid" might pass into the body of his patients. 

DR. BRAID'S METHOD. 

Dr. Braid held or placed some bright object before and slightly above the eyes of his patients. He then commanded them to gaze intently and fixedly at this object, and to concentrate their attention directly upon it until the eyes grew tired, and as he supposed hypnosis was induced because of this. 

Ernest Jendrasik of Buda Pesth has expressed the opinion that fixed attention is only effectual because it causes fatigue to the optic nerve, and consequently produces insensibility to external sense-stimulation. It is a well-known fact among Psychologists that for the induction for ordinary sleep it is not absolutely necessary that all of the functions of the brain should be tired, and if any one be sufficiently tired, either physically or mentally, sleep will ensue. The strained position of the eyes, and their convergence, under Dr. 

Braid's method would, undoubtedly, very soon fatigue the optic nerve, and thus sleep would be induced. 

Dr. Braid used this method exclusively, and, consid-

ering that he held materialistic views, he had fairly good success.

PASSES. 

Magnetic passes were in the past considered of great importance by Psychologists and Hypnotists, who were convinced that there was a magnetic influence or an invisible influence transmitted from the operator to the subject, and the investigator will find the most minute directions given for the correct application and use of said passes. The "Pass" proper, however, was never used by Mesmer, to whom it was unknown, strange as this statement may sound to those who have a slight knowledge of his methods.

Every operator should gaze fixedly into the eyes of his subject as long as he continues to make the passes. 

The will is an essential element, and is a necessary factor in the curing of disease and inducing Hypnosis, therefore the operator should strongly exercise his will that the nerve force or magnetism may pass and be directed from his body into that of the patient or subject. This requires a positive condition of mind, and is used for the sole purpose of enveloping the patient or subject in a healthy magnetism or a sleepy influence as the operator desires. Here the writer desires to state that it is of the utmost importance that the student understands that this magnetism, Personal influence or Vital soul essence which is transmitted or projected from the operator to subject takes the very nature and condition of mind entertained and maintained by the operator, viz., if the operator thinks sleep he transmits a sleepy influence, etc., while holding a concentration upon the subject. The modern psychologists realize that there

is great and wonderful efficacy in the "Pass" where the same is made by strong concentration of the will. 

The positive Pass, or more properly curative magnetic Pass, for toothache, neuralgia, rheumatism, Spinal complaint, etc., is in cases of this sort very valuable. Ch. 

Richet, Professor of Physiology at the University of Paris, uses this method in preference to any other. 

The author has found by personal experience it is very effective in every case. The precise nature of the action of these passes is not essential. Of course, verbal suggestion should be used in conjunction with passes while inducing Hypnosis or curing disease. Again, if the Hypnotist takes hold of the hands of the subject or patient there is an emanation of this magnetism, and the subject will feel a strange sensation or influence, which at times induces an inhibitory action in the delicate nerves of the eye and mouth.

BERGER'S METHOD. 

Berger of Breslau states that in many cases he has through silent concentration found this influence which is transmitted from subject to operator sufficient to induce a profound degree of Hypnosis, and in many cases it was only necessary to pass the hands silently down. before the face of the subject, all the while keeping an intense concentration upon the subject. 

This theory certainly supports the explanation of the passes which has been previously stated in this work. 

Another hypothesis is that a magnetic action is caused. 

De Tarchanoff has shown that by systematic and gentle stimulation of the skin by strokes and passes that a mag. netic current passes from the subject to the operator

and is produced by a strong concentration of the will, and in consequence of which concentration, muscular contraction always ensues. All Psychologists and students of mental science hold as the result of direct observation and personal experience that a strong volitional effort is absolutely necessary, and experience will serve to explain the value and great importance of this exertion of the will.

Pitres of Bordeaux states that certain portions of the body are more particularly sensitive to magnetic influence than the other. Many operators by exercising the will in conjunction with verbal suggestion have obtained a Lethargic condition; this experiment is always convincing, as the mental effort cannot well be excluded in any case where results are expected or desired. 

METHOD OF FASCINATING. 

Fascination always introduces a large amount of the personal magnetism or life element of the operator, and is a favorite one by those who prefer the silent mental suggestions to the verbal.

Subjects are instructed to gaze steadily into the operator's eyes until hypnosis is induced. Sometimes it happens that in a short space of time the subject will imitate each and every movement of the Hypnotist, all the while keeping his eyes firmly fixed upon those of the operator. Revolving hand tests, etc., can be given by using this method, which excludes all verbal suggestions.

WARNING. 

This method of fascination is more or less risky, since, if the subject be positive or refractory, the Hypnotist,

be he not equally as positive, may himself involuntarily become hypnotized by self induction. Lloyd Tuckey records instances, where, in using this method, he found himself developing the first symptoms of Hypnosis. 

LASEGUE'S METHOD. 

Lasegue, a prominent physician of Paris, found that by closing the eyes and then making passes and a moderate pressure upon the eye-balls for some minutes

Hypnosis was induced, but this, of course, must not be expected only in a very sensitive subject. 

FARIA'S METHOD. 

The Abbe Faria always concentrated his attention upon the subject as much as possible and after some minutes of perfect silence would suddenly shout in a loud and commanding voice the word "sleep." In some cases this was sufficient to obtain the desired result. 

MAGNETS. 

Several so-called authorities claim that the magnet has in some cases the power to induce Hypnosis. 

Ballet, Binet, Fere, Landowzy, and Proust, all of Paris, and Benedict, of Vienna, hold this view. 

After many careful experiments the writer can confidently say that he has been unable to find any trace of such influence, and this result is confirmed by the almost universal testimony of Hypnotists in all parts of the world.

It may be that in a certain few cases where verbal suggestions or expectancy is present that the magnet has been used as a material means to induce Hypnosis. 

In all cases it is a natural hypothesis to attribute the

induction of Hypnosis to suggestion, either mental or verbal, which certainly enters into every method, and which is so settled in its action that it is almost impossible in any case for an operator to positively exclude or entirely avoid it. Dr. Braid has left on record an experiment of his which bears on the supposed influence of the magnet. A certain lady told him that she could not endure a magnet being brought near her, and that it always had the most profound influence on her; and so it did, when she knew of its proximity; but Dr. Braid, in order to test the nature of this influence which was supposed by her to emanate from the magnet, sat next to her on one occasion for over an hour, with a powerful magnet concealed on his person, and, as he expected, found that no effect whatever was produced upon the lady.

DRUGS. 

Chanbard places the anaesthetics, chloroform, ether, and other similar drugs amongst the agents for producing Hypnosis.

That, in some cases, a receptivity to suggestion occurs in the state produced by the action of drugs is undeniable; but the same may be said of certain forms of ordinary sleep, and it seems an unnecessary confusion of the subject to regard these analogous states as being of an 

Hypnotic origin.

LUY'S "MIRROR ROTAIF.” 

This is an ingenious instrument invented by Dr.

Luy of the Charity Hospital, Paris. It consists of two mirrors, which rapidly revolve in opposite directions, and by gazing at this for a short space of time the sub-

ject becomes tired and dazed, and Hypnosis is easily produced by concentration and verbal suggestions from the operator. Many advantages are claimed for this. method—that it saves the operator time and trouble, and is impersonal; that a number of people can be hypnotized at the same time by it, and that it never fails. 

The saving of time is not really so great as might be imagined, for each subject must certainly need the Hypnotist's attention, whilst there are many other methods equally impersonal. Again, only fairly susceptible persons and those who have been previously hypnotized would, generally speaking, be influenced en masse, and these could be Hypnotized just as quickly by almost any other means, especially direct mental concentration. 

The mirror is, however, of some service in certain cases. No practiced Hypnotist adheres rigidly to any one given means or method. Experience teaches him that where it is the brain that is most intimately concerned, the idiosyncrasies and character of each individual must be studied, and a means or method selected which seems most likely to bring about the desired results.

In all of the little differences of details, however, there is one main principle now recognized by every wise student of practical psychology, and this is the theory of mental suggestion.

Abbe Faria, in 1814, apparently, as far as the writer is able to discern, made certain advances toward the elucidation of this principle. The author in 1899 by his insistence upon the necessity of mental suggestion, combined with fixed attention, attracted attention from medical men and the scientific world in general.

To him belongs the honor of giving to the Western world a scientific exposition of the rationale of silent mental concentration.

THE DE LAURENCE METHODS. 

The subject or patient, as it may be, is of course comfortably seated in a reclining position, where the light, if any, will not affect his eyes. Then the hypnotist takes a position directly in front of the individual whom he intends either to influence or place asleep, concentrating his gaze directly upon the right or left pupil of his subject's eye. Remember, students, that you can never use but one pupil of the eyes at a given time; choose either the right or left, it matters not, but it is of the greatest importance that you concentrate your energy, will power, soul essence or vital magnetism upon one of the pupils of your subject or patient. The patients should be instructed to look directly into the eyes of the operator, and as much as possible to keep their mind concentrated upon the intention and desire of going to sleep.

The operator has upon his part at this very moment responsibilities that are of the utmost importance if he desires results.

He must gaze intensely into the selected pupil of the eye of his subject; if he desires to induce sleep he must within himself think sleep-sleep-with all the intensity of his soul, keeping said concentration for about two minutes. At the expiration of this time the eyes usually begin to show a decided trance-like stare or condition of complete fascination. Then the Hypnotist should begin in a muffled and monotonous tone of voice to sug-

gest sleep, stating to the subject that they feel a drowsy, sleepy sensation or strange condition passing all over their body; that their eyes are getting heavy and sleepy, and that they will directly pass into a deep, sound sleep. Of course, certain judgment must be displayed by every operator as the symptoms or oncoming of Hypnosis begin to develop themselves. 

Again, it must not be supposed that in each and every case the same method, formula or detail of treatment must be used or followed, but the principle nevertheless is the same.

In other words, the operator must concentrate his whole mind, attention and will power upon the subject with intensity, then he must give both verbal and mental suggestions to produce or induce in his subject or patient that which he desires. As a matter of fact, there is no one given method which will succeed in all cases; some subjects or patients will be quite uninfluenced by one method of treatment or induction, whilst they will be readily susceptible to another. The writer has succeeded in Hypnotizing by an intense silent mental concentration, combined with "Passes," where the Braid method of fixed attention and verbal suggestion both failed.

The method usually adopted by the writer differs materially from the Nancy or Braid method for inducing Hypnosis as above described; therefore, he will here incorporate into the description details of the utmost importance to the student. The first essential for the successful induction of Hypnosis in a person who has not previously been hypnotized is to insure that the person is in a condition both mental and physical which

is quite comfortable, and one that he will be able to maintain during the period of induction without any discomfort; every small detail in this respect is an important feature in determining the degree of success or failure on the part of the operator in a large number of subjects; the style of chair in which the patient or subject rests; its position in relation to the lighting of the room; the position of the limbs and feet, hands and arms; the head, whenever supported, should not be permitted to drop backward, and the subject should in every case be made as comfortable as possible. Environment must be entirely free from any disquieting conditions, influences, and audible sounds, which would necessarily serve to disturb or attract the attention of the subject.

The confidential whisper which some take such delight in, moving about the room, etc., should not be tolerated. 

These conditions generally serve to disturb the attention of the subject or patient at the oncoming or induction of Hynosis. The person operated upon should also be asked to eliminate every thought foreign to sleep from his mind as far as possible; he should also be told not to concern himself regarding any method or means used by the Hypnotist; not to pay attention to what he may say, and especially not to attempt by any mental effort to assist the operator in trying to go to sleep. 

Every precaution should also be taken to see that the patient is entirely free from nervousness. Then place the subject in a comfortable chair, and the next point is to attract his attention. 

For this purpose it is not theoretically necessary that the operator should resort to verhal suggestion, as the

attention is fixed much more easily by the intense snake like gleam in the operator's eyes who uses mental suggestions. This means never fails to gain the attention and fixation of the sight, and is the very best and readiest means to cause a subject to look steadily and without blinking more than can be helped. The exact distance from the subject matters little; it may be two feet, ten or twenty. The hours after day has closed are, on the whole, to be greatly preferred, for during the day, and especially during the morning, the nervous irritability is decidedly greater than in the evening; therefore the subject is consequently rendered passive and susceptible with ease, and his general condition, both mental and physical, is far more favorable. After dinner most people are disposed to remain quietly sitting in a chair for a short period, whereas, earlier in the day an enforced restraint would necessarily be more or less troublesome. 

Of course it should not be assumed that all observances or details are necessary in every case, for it certainly depends upon the susceptibility of the person, but the operator who desires an average of anything over ninety per cent will profit by close attention to the above details. 

The first induction of sleep is generally the most difficult; after that certain precautions can be generally dispensed with. The expression that comes into the subject's eyes will be quite interesting. Perfectly passive, with his trance-like gaze riveted upon the eyes of the hypnotist, completely fascinated, he now gradually passes from the normal to a Hypnotic state; after an interval of varying duration a decided change takes place. The author will not attempt to describe this change accurately in so many words, but the experienced hypnotist recognizes it

instantly; the pupils are intensely dilated; the eyelids are heavy and quivering; the subject, in plain words, is completely fascinated; his face has lost its ordinary expression; the respiratory rhythm is considerably altered.

At this stage the hypnotist must close in, for everything now depends upon his keeping an intense concentration on the subject, all the while willing him into a deep sleep. 

Accurate perception of the changes which indicate the oncoming of hypnosis is absolutely essential. The decided and characteristic reaction of the subject's mind to mental suggestion is beginning. The operator must judge the proper time to intensely will the eyes to close. 

If he begins too early, he will fatigue himself; if he waits until too late, as many do, the subject will return, more or less, to the normal condition and the operator has missed his best opportunity to induce Hypnosis. 

The operator's expression and attitude must not be of a character repellent to the subject's consciousness. 

For the benefit of the student the writer will again say that the symptoms of hypnosis are as follows: "The eyelids are quivering; the eyes are tired; the subject feels a sleepy, drowsy influence creeping over his body; the eyes close and sleep is come." Gradually the state has diverged more and more from the waking; the final hypnosis usually comes suddenly. After the heavy, sleepy eyelids have closed, one symptom is always present-a strange, peculiar inspiration, which is so characteristic with the phenomena of hypnosis induced by the above method.

The inductive stages may therefore be classified as: 

1. PASSIVITY. 

2. PASSIVITY WITH ATTENTION.

3. ACUTE PASSIVITY WITH ACUTE ATTEN- 

TION. 

4. COMPLETE FASCINATION. 

5. HYPNOSIS. 

The degree of sleep obtained by any of the various methods used varies with each and every individual, but there are certain stages or classifications which are important; some subjects pass into the light stage; others into a deep degree; as a rule the state of hypnosis deepens with every induction until about the seventh or eighth; by this time every subject will have reached their deepest degree; in the inductions subsequent to these they present decided phenomena of the deepest stages. This is a strange, curious, but constant phenomena and every operator should classify each subject according to his stage and induction, a point which, in experimental work by the student, is exceedingly useful. 

Said stages or degrees always vary from a condition which only an experienced operator can recognize as a hypnotic condition, in which decided abnormal phenomena are ever present. The variety of these stages is so great that many medical men and operators have endeavored to classify them.

They, of course, are useful in giving the student an idea of the wonderful differences between the degrees of hypnosis obtained with different subjects. Therefore, the writer will give them. Gurney, whose researches are valuable, though speculative, divides Hypnosis into two stages: 

1. The "alert" stage. 

2. The "deep" stage.

HYPNOTISM AND MAGNETIS 

68 

Forel names three states: 

1. Drowsiness. 

2. Inability to open the eyes. Obedience to suggestion. 

3. Somnambulism. Loss of memory. 

Lloyd Tuckey gives a similar classification to Forel's: 

1. Light sleep. 

2. Profound sleep. 

3. Somnambulism. 

Liebeault has described six different stages: 

1. Drowsiness. 

2. Drowsiness. Suggestive catalepsy possible. 

3. Light sleep. Automatic movements possible. 

4. Deep sleep. The subject ceases to be in relation with the outer world. 

5. Light somnambulism. Memory, on waking, indistinct and hazy. 

6. Deep Somnambulism. Entire loss of memory on waking. All the phenomena of post-hypnotic suggestion. 

Bernheim suggests no less than nine divisions: 

1. Drowsiness. Suggestions of local warmth are effective. 

2. Drowsiness with inability to open the eyes. 

3. Suggestive catalepsy slightly present. 

4. Suggestive catalepsy more profound. 

5. Suggestive contractures may be induced. 

6. Automatic obedience. 

7. Loss of memory on waking. Hallucinations not possible. 

8. Loss of memory. Slight possibility of producing hallucinations, but not post-hypnotical. 

9. Loss of memory. Hypnotic and post-hypnotic hallucinations possible.

The writer will here submit for the student an analysis of Liebeault's which is very interesting:

Deep somnambulism Uninfluenced 33 100 460 230 31 131 22 1,012 These various classifications are, however, extremely unsatisfactory. The terms "drowsiness" and "sleep" should certainly be excluded from an accurate differentiation, and "Somnambulism," a word which already has a definite implication in another sense, should also be excluded.

During Hypnosis the extent to which verbal or mental suggestion affects the subject depends upon the extent to which he is divorced from consciousness of the external, and on the degree to which the psychical action of the neuronic groups are inhibited. In the deeper stages, where there is absence of memory on waking, this divorce is complete, and the particular phenomena of the deeper stages depend upon that psychological con dition or mental state which predominates over the ner、 vous system. To be concise, different stages may be classified as: 

1. Light Hypnosis. 

External consciousness present. 

External consciousness slightly present.

2. Deep Hypnosis. 

3. Profound Hypnosis.

It is useless to attempt more detailed classification than this.

Thus in Bernheim's stage 3, the phenomena of stages 5 and 6 may easily be obtained, the degree of sugges tive catalepsy putting it in stage 3, the other phenomena putting it in these later stages. 

Moreover, many post-hypnotic suggestions obtain their reactions almost as well in the lightest stages as in the deepest, provided the suggestion be of a character to suit the condition. 

In the medical application of hypnosis the use of the light stages in this respect is well known; the phenomena of hallucinations and other phenomena requiring wellmarked concomitant changes in the nervous system are naturally confined to the stages of profound hypnosis. 

The de-hypnotization, or waking from hypnosis, is effected by suggestion, on the same principle as that on which the state is induced. Physical means, such as blowing on the eyes, may be used; but, in any case, they can only be regarded as aids to suggestion, and their value depends entirely on the mental impression they produce.

Many means are recommended by various writers for waking the patient; fanning, sprinkling with water, loud calls and noise, etc. According to Pitres and others, there are certain parts of the body where stimulation has an awakening effect; to these they have given the name, "Zones hypno-fernatrices."

The forcible opening of the eyes will sometimes awaken the patient. The use of these physical means,

however, are quite unnecessary and should certainly be avoided by the Psychologists who realize that these means simply act as the result of suggestion. 

WAKING A SUBJECT. 

It should be distinctly understood that there is no danger whatever in the operator being unable to awaken his subject, for in every instance the sleep, whether induced by fixation of gaze, verbal suggestion, concentration or any other means or methods, can be instantly terminated if the operator will maintain a calm and fearless condition of mind toward his subject. Of course it will be readily seen that confidence is absolutely essential. When ready to awaken a subject, the operator should give suggestions as follows: "Now, when I count three you will find that you no longer have any desire to remain asleep, but will want to wake up; you will find that it will be an impossibility for you to keep an sleeping after I count three."

When these suggestions have been given in a decided tone of voice, clap the hands at the side of the subject's head and he will be wide awake. Under the control of an ignorant operator, however, the subject is liable to pass into a condition similar to lethargy, and completely out from under the control of the amateur, who will be unable to influence or awaken his patient. Therefore the importance of retaining at all times a confident condition of mind can not be too strongly insisted upon. 

If the operator should have the misfortune to lose control of his subject entirely he should go out of the room and away from the subject, whose nervousness would necessarily be increased by his presence. If possible an

experienced operator should be called in, but if this is not possible, remove all disturbing influences from around the subject and he will invariably sleep off the hypnctic influence. In the cases of this kind which have come under the writer's care, more harm had been done the subject by the operator insisting and attempting to arouse the subject. These ignorant and futile attempts did the patient more harm than anything else. One case in particular which the writer feels will interest the student was that of a young man who was hypnotized by a friend at an evening party, for fun. The operator was an amateur, and the first attempt produced trembling throughout the whole body; a second trial the patient fell on the floor, jumped up, became merry, laughed, joked and sang; then violent convulsions set in, followed by loss of speech; catalepsy next appeared, and the whole body, except the arms, became rigid. For two days convulsions, loss of speech and catalepsy recurred very frequently, and the sight of anything bright excited the man to madness. Various sedatives were tried, and for ten days his condition was not much improved, but after this the severe symptoms began to give way to outbursts of alternate singing and lamenting, and after another period of twelve days, during which time his temperature went up to 103 degrees, the young man recovered, but operations of this kind are not necessary.

DURATION OF SLEEP. 

The length of time which a subject would remain asleep if not awakened by the operator differs, of course, with each subject. Some have been known to wake at the very moment when the operator leaves them, the fact

of his absence acting as a suggestion that they are no longer under his control. Others, if they have been given no positive suggestion to remain asleep, will be awakened by an unexpected or loud noise.

Some will be roused from the state by efforts made in it; thus, for instance, a subject has been awakened by laughing loudly in obedience to a suggestion from the operator. If the sleep be very deep it may continue for six or seven hours, but if light, the subjects usually return to the normal condition in a short space of time. 

The writer knows of many cases where the subject has slept from 18 to 24 hours. After being hypnotized by a competent operator no person ever experiences any bad results, such as "sleepiness," or "giddiness," and ill effects, if any, are due entirely to the fault of the operator. Different ideas have been expressed, some—well, many-ill-informed, with reference to the persons who are hypnotizable. It would be idle to affirm of any particular temperament that inclines itself to hypnosis when it has been found that over ninety per cent of all persons tried is the "minimum" number of any who properly understand the science in its practical application. 

Speaking from his own efforts, the writer has found that the class presenting the least resistance and generally giving satisfactory experimental inductions, is to be found in young men of education and of fairly all-round mental development; such a man, for instance, as is typical of Oxford or Cambridge. 

Excessive self-consciousness presents some difficulty, and consequently the more or less brilliant neurotic and the very stupid and vain resemble one another in being difficult to influence.

The deaf and dumb are not hypnotizable, and the insane are not easily influenced. Sex does not seem to in any way affect the induction. There is a somewhat common misconception prevalent which regards hysterical conditions as likely to indicate easy hypnotizability. 

Hysteria, however, is nearly always the cause of much difficulty and never makes the induction easy. 

Nationality has very little to do with the science. Taking nationalities of very different dispositions, we find difference in the recorded experiments. 

In France Liebeault hypnotized 1,850 out of 2,000; in

Sweden Wellenstrand hypnotized 957 out of 980, and in Holland Van Rentezhen hypnotized 497 out of 500. 

Bernheim and Forel agree, with reference to the medical application of hypnosis, that the opinion of physicians who cannot hypnotize at least 80 per cent of their patients is of no value. The present writer's percentage in all cases between January, 1892, and December, 1896, was 915. Amongst members of the Universities his percentage was 95.84. And judging from his observation of a large number of cases he is in every sense inclined to regard susceptibility to hypnosis as generally belonging to intelligent men and women; unquestionably the process of hypnotizing refined people is easier, and, as a rule, takes less exertion. Reference should here be made to conditions of hypnosis not recognized by most authorities, but described originally by Charcot and upheld by some of his pupils. These states are not, in the writer's opinion, at all representative of normal hypnosis, but, if accurately described, are pathological curiosities due to the peculiar material used for the experiments. We append an extract from Charcot's description of these states:

DE LAURENCE METHOD. 

1. THE CATALEPTIC STATE.-This may be pro duced: (a) primarily, under the influence of an intense and unexpected noise, of a bright light presented to the gaze, or, again, in some subjects, by the more or less prolonged fixing of the eyes on a given object; (b) consequently to the lethargic state, when the eyes, which up to that moment had been closed, are exposed to the light by raising the eyelids.

The subject thus rendered cataleptic is motionless and, as it were, fascinated. The eyes are open, the gaze is fixed, the eyelids do not quiver, and every muscle is rigid throughout the body. Often there is anesthesia of the conjunctia, and even of the cornea. 

The limbs and all parts of the subject's body will retain the position in which it has been placed for a considerable period, even when the attitude is one which is difficult to maintain. The limbs are extremely heavy when raised or displaced, and there is flexibilitis cerea, or what is termed the stiffness of a lay figure. 

While there is complete insensibility to pain, some of the senses retain their activity, at any rate in part. 

The method in common use at the De Laurence Institute of Hypnotism and Occult Philosophy is as follows: 

After the subject has been placed in a deep sleep by the methods given above, the operator should instruct him that when he counts three he will stand upon his feet, and when the operator places both of his thumbs at the base of the subject's brain, where it joins the spine, the subject will feel every nerve and muscle in his body get rigid as a piece of steel; that his arms,

limbs, back and neck will get so rigid that they cannot be bent, and that when he is placed with his head upon one chair and his feet upon the back of another, his body while in this condition will sustain any weight placed upon it. These, of course, are post-hypnotic suggestions in a sense, and must be repeated three or four times in a firm and decided manner, while the subject is in a deep state of hypnosis; then, when he has been made to stand upon his feet the operator must take a position directly behind him, placing thumbs at the base of the brain, fingers and hands pressing either side of the head; now here is a critical point, and the operator must hold an intense concentration on the subject about where the spine joins the brain, holding one thought in his mind-that is, that the subject will become rigid as steel all over his body. Now in connection with the concentration give verbal suggestions. 

In a deep and determined voice say "rigid," "rigid, rigid," you feel every part of your body get rigid. If the operator has properly followed out the directions given here, there will, after the suggestions have been given, a convulsive shiver pass through the body of the subject, and instantly his or her body will become rigid.

When this has been produced quickly take a position at the right side of the subject, place the left hand upon the back of his stiffened neck, have an assistant take hold of his rigid limbs, then together lift the rigid body to the backs of two chairs, which should, of course, have been previously arranged for this purpose. 

Have a third chair set beside the now rigid form, step quickly upon it, and from there place your right foot, without any hesitation whatever, upon the subject;

have your assistant standing upon the opposite side, let him grasp your right hand so that he may assist you to get upon the subject's body without any sudden pressure.

In selecting an assistant if possible get a tall person, as they can reach your hand better. But after you have taken a standing position upon the subject's body let go of your assistant's hand, and with one foot upon the breast and the other upon the limbs, half way between the knees and hips, you will be able to balance yourself without trouble. Keep this standing position upon the subject no longer than five or six seconds, then have your assistant take your right hand and lightly spring from the body of your subject to the floor. 

Now the operator should take hold of the shoulders of the subject, the assistant take his limbs, and stand him upon his feet. Then give the following suggestions: 

Now, when I count three and clap my hands, you will become "wide awake." Your body will instantly relax all over, and you will wake up. The operator in giving this test must bear in mind that it is absolutely necessary that he have assistants for holding chairs, etc., who understand their business. Again, this work must be all done in a businesslike and rapid manner. If this is carried out no bad results whatever will follow, as the writer has many times taken young ladies of slight physique, not weighing over one hundred pounds, and made their body so rigid that they would easily bear the weight of men weighing over one hundred and eighty.

SOMNAMBULISM. 

DE LAURENCE'S METHODS. 

This trancelike condition is produced as follows:

After inducing Hypnosis, raise the subject's right arm, give positive suggestions that the arm is becoming rigid and so stiffened at the elbow that it cannot be bent, or that the subject, no matter how hard he may try, cannot take his arm down from its upright position until told to do so by the operator. If hypnosis has been actually induced it will be impossible for the subject to take down his arm, no matter how much he may attempt to do so. The arm may be placed in this position for over half an hour and the subject will remain sound asleep, apparently perfectly unconscious of the position of his arm. 

After this has been accomplished give suggestions that when you count three the subject will take down his arm, but will go sounder and deeper asleep. That he cannot wake up or come from under the influence until told to do so by the hypnotist, but will keep going deeper and deeper asleep. Now say to the the subject, "When I count five you will be able to open your eyes, but will still remain sound asleep." 

Repeat these suggestions three or four times; then say to the subject, "Now, when I count five you will open your eyes, but will not wake up." Now, here is where any delusion or hallucination desired can be induced by verbal suggestion. Give the subject any delusion you like; tell him he is a dog, and he will bark.

Tell him he is a nurse and put some article in his arms for a baby, and he will make every attempt to

quiet the supposed child, especially if he is told that it is crying. Tell him he is some famous musician; then set him down to an ordinary table, leading him to believe the same is a piano, and he will play upon the table.

Impress upon his mind the fact that he is a stylishly dressed young lady, and he will carry out the same. 

While in this condition he will fight, cry, laugh, dance, or do any other ridiculous thing that the operator may care to have him. When it is desired to bring the subject from under the influence, place him asleep again, tell him that when you count three he will be "wide awake," that he will have no memory or recollection of anything that has happened or that he has done while asleep. Then proceed to wake him up by counting three and clapping your hands.

It is difficult to analyze the very complex phenomena which are presented in this trancelike condition. It seems plain, however, that more importance is being attached to Somnambulism since it has been studied from a psychological point of view.

CHAPTER IV. 

Nervous System-Hypnotic Suggestion-Respiration and Pulsa tion-The Hæmic Murmur of Anæmia-Acute Passivity-The 

Eyes and the Mouth-Paralysis of a Leg or Foot-Suggestive Intensification Besides Speech-Muscular Contraction-Motor Impulses-Practical Psychology-The Psychical Phenomena —Independent Ideation-Abnormal Phenomena-Retention of Ideas-Lighter Stages of Hypnosis-In the Deepest States- The Effects of Hypnotic Suggestion in Increasing the Retentive Powers-Loss of Memory Can Be Induced-Motor 

Disturbances-Richet-Hypnosis-Waking State-Post-Hyp notic-Hallucinations and Illusions-Auto-Suggestion-Hypnotic Hallucination-The Hallucinations and Illusions of Light-The Increase of the Faculty of Sight-Color and Color 

Blindness.

The characteristic feature of hypnosis is the presence of a condition in which a suggestion causes a reaction of the central nervous system defintely corresponding with the nature of the suggestion; underlying this bald statement are some complex problems, which we shall discuss in their proper place, and in this chapter we shall confine our attention, as far as possible, to a description of the hypnotic phenomena, leaving the etiological questions

thus involved for separate treatment. All the phenomena here to be discussed range themselves then either as phenomena of hypnosis itself or as phenomena dependent upon hypnotic suggestion.

The alterations of the ordinary functions of the body during hypnosis vary in their degree and number in accordance with the method of hypnotization. Some subjects are able to move with perfect ease and freedom; thus they will brush a fly off the face with the hand, or change their position when one posture becomes wearying. Others present to the superficial observer all the appearances of being in a heavy sleep. The breathing is slower and deeper than in the normal condition, the inspirations being full and prolonged; the pulse is somewhat slower, but increased in fullness and strength; in both respiration and pulsation there is an increased regularity. During the passage of the subject from the normal to the hypnotic condition the signs are not so stant; but, as a rule, one finds a slight both pulsation and respiration. con- acceleration of

At the moment when the subject passes into hypnosis there is always, more or less marked, a deep inspiration Female... Male.... Male. Female.

of peculiar quality.* The hæmic murmur of anæmia has a curious tendency in some cases to diminish or disappear in hypnosis; whilst mitral systolic, aortic diastolic, and other murmurs of endocarditic origin appear usually to be unaffected. The general condition of the subject when hypnosis has been induced is one of acute passivity; the position which the patient has taken up may remain the same down to the smallest detail for the space of half an hour or more; the fingers, clasped or open, will so remain. If at the beginning one foot has been slightly inverted, in half an hour's time it will be inverted to the same degree; if the head has drooped to one side, though the attitude be one seriously uncomfortable for a person in the normal state to maintain, it will remain in the same position. The subject can hear perfectly well; but the most amusing story may be told to others in the room without provoking the slightest reaction in him. 

The normal irritability of the nervous system has dis- * The mitral presystolic is certainly affected by hypnosis; in one case under the writer's observation it regularly disappeared in hypnosis, and on careful auscultation one could hear the gradual diminution; it generally took about three minutes to disappear entirely; but for the last two minutes it could hardly have been recognized had it not been previously heard. In another case a presystolic murmur developed in hypnosis; this murmur, however, was not new, as the subject had had it for two years previously, if not longer; in subsequent hypnoses its appearance was typically irregular; on more than one occasion it was present in the normal state immediately before hypnosis, and disappeared in hypnosis. The hæmic murmurs of anæmia are, however, frequent in their disappearance, though in a good many cases of this class they may remain unaltered. In a case recently under observation a girl aged twenty-three had a marked systolic murmur, heard at the apex and the base, and in both subclavian arteries, with the venous hum well developed; after the subject had been in hypnosis five minutes the venous hum had almost disappeared, and the murmur at the apex and in the subclavian arteries was absent; at the base it was still heard, though much diminished.

appeared in a striking manner, and the stimuli provoking a reaction in the case of a person in the normal condition will produce no effect on him when hypnotized. 

The eyes and the mouth often show striking symptoms of the onset of hypnosis. The closing of the eyes is generally preceded by a marked quivering of the eyelids, and these vibrations are often maintained for some time after the eyes have closed in sleep.

It is not always necessary that the eyes should be closed for a person to be hypnotized, though this is generally the case, and in many instances to open the eyes would awaken the subject. When the method of fascination is used the eyes may remain wide open the whole time. In the deepest hypnosis it is frequently found that the eyes are not quite closed, but that there is a slight opening left, through which the eyeball can be seen.

Sometimes, as the eyes close, the eyeball turns upwards and remains in this position till the subject is awakened; at other times it is found that the eyeball returns to its natural position when the eyes are closed. 

Perhaps a more subtle symptom is the strange effect which the induction of hypnosis produces on the delicate muscles of the mouth. This symptom is one of the most constant, and may be observed more or less marked in almost every case, whether the hypnosis induced be light or deep. Often the symptom is only recognizable by a slight compression of the lips; but frequently the contraction of the muscles is so obvious that the subject appears to be indulging in a grim smile. 

In any but the lightest stages of hypnosis all muscular movements can be prevented or induced by means of suggestion. Thus the statement, "You cannot open your

mouth," or "You cannot bend your arm, is sufficient to prevent the action being performed. 99 The subject may be unable to make the movement because he cannot contract the proper muscles; whilst in other cases the suggestion causes a contracture of the antagonistic muscles, and thus the forbidden movement becomes impossible. The simple inability is frequently seen when the suggestion, "You cannot open your eyes,' is made. Unable to contract the proper muscles, the subject attempts to open his eyes by elevating his eyebrows. 

The course of the suggestion in these cases is different, and depends upon the nervous system of the subject. In the case of simple inability the suggestion acts as a direct inhibition of motor impulses of the proper neuronic group; in the case of antagonistic contracture the suggestion is expressed in physical terms by the action of the opposing group.

It is also possible to allow or prevent contraction for any one particular purpose. A subject, in response to the proper suggestion, will be able to say some definite word, but in all other respects will be absolutely dumb. 

He will be able to write, play upon any musical instrument, sew; but quite unable to hold in his hand some given object.

As any muscular contraction can be inhibited so a contraction can be enforced. Thus the suggestion may be made that it is impossible for him to prevent his arm rising to a level with his shoulder; if there be any effort on the part of the subject to prevent this, it is on account of some suggestion that he should resist. The suggestion just mentioned contains the elements of such an additional intimation.

PHENOMENA OF HYPNUTISM. 

85 By means of suggestion it is possible to compel the subject to cough, sneeze, laugh, weep, etc., etc. Paralysis of a leg or a foot, an arm or a hand, can be induced in the same way. In the case of those subjects who are in a deep stage, a series of movements will be performed by them if they be so directed.

It is important to note that while suggestion may be made, and usually is made, verbally to the subject, it is by no means necessary that words should be used. All that is requisite is that the subject should clearly understand what it is that is desired of him. The organs of sense and perception are all channels for the conveyance of any suggestion made to the subject. It is found, for instance, that some action on the part of the hypnotist will tend to bring the suggestion more vividly before the mind of the patient.

This fact led many to suppose that the physical action of the operator had some intrinsic value; such is, however, not the case; its only value lies in its power of intensifying the impression which it is desired to create. 

The professional magnetizers have been very fond of a particular method which is called, amongst other names, "imitation," "fascination," and "Donatism" (this last from Donato, who made great use of it). In this system the operator fixes his eyes on the eyes of the subject, and after a short time the subject follows every movement made by the magnetist. If he lift an arm, the subject does the same; if he kneel, the subject kneels; and so on ad infinitum. Here fascination was the form of hypnosis induced. The same state can be obtained by opening the eyes of an hypnotized person, when the hypnotist, by

gazing fixedly into his subject's eyes, will be able to obtain these imitative movements. If the finger, or the mounted top of a walking-stick, be placed before the subject's eyes he will follow the finger, or the stick, as the case may be; in all this it is clearly suggestion which is the basis of the phenomena. The subject will not perform any of these imitative actions, nor will he be "fascinated" by the stick, unless he fully understands that it is expected of him. 

In very many ways, by a look or a movement, the hypnotist is often able to convey a suggestion to his subject which will be quite as potent as if made by means of speech. This extreme susceptibility to suggestion is either not known or is overlooked by the ordinary public, and the professional hypnotizers often avail themselves of this common ignorance to deceive those who may attend their exhibitions. 

There is a particular form of suggestion which acts, in the first place, on what has been termed the muscular sense. By this term is meant that faculty which tells us, without the necessity of looking, the position of our limbs. It causes a peculiar phenomenon in hypnosis. The arm of a subject under hypnosis is raised by the hypnotist and then let go. Instead of falling to his side the arm remains fixed in the position in which the hypnotist left it. In other cases, however, the subject will let his arm fall until the hypnotist gives the verbal suggestion that it shall remain rigid. The important point to be noted is that, in each case, suggestion was the cause of this catalepsy; only in the former where the state was deeper and the subject in consequence more receptive of suggestion, it was sufficient to act on the muscular sense

alone, whilst in the latter it was necessary to intensify the suggestion by speech. Of course, no other means could be used for the suggestive intensification besides speech; if the arm were held in one position for some minutes by the hypnotist, or a pass were made along the arm, the intended suggestion would probably take effect. 

It has been said that these muscular phenomena of contraction must be distinguished from the suggestion which induces them, and, certainly, the examination of any catalepsed limb is sufficient to show that in consequence of the suggestion there has been a striking physical change; but it is also claimed that in some cases the contraction is so great that a contrary suggestion will not suffice to counteract the effect of the first. The writer has observed numerous cases of the most rigid catalepsy, but this failure of the second suggestion is quite unknown to him; though frequently there is a delay of a few seconds before the arm regains its proper elasticity. This, however, seems explained by the auto-suggestion which the contracted state of the muscle would naturally have on the mind of the subject. 

If, for example, the subject be prevented from shutting his mouth, when the contrary suggestion is made, the mouth will shut with a snap, clearly showing the suddenness with which the efferent impulses have ceased. 

Another common phenomenon of the state is that called "automatic" or "continued movement." If the subject's arms be rotated, or his hands set revolving round one another, they will continue to rotate or revolve. This again is due to an impression made on the subject, by virtue of which the result is precisely the same as if a verbal suggestion had been made to him.

Various and strange automatic movements will be continued by the subject, when they have been once started by the hypnotist, such, for instance, as rising from his chair and then sitting down, working the head backwards and forwards, opening and shutting the mouth, etc., etc.

The subject will even continue to walk, if he be properly started.

In some few cases an extremely lethargic state is found in which the subject is apparently almost impervious to suggestion, and appears to be in a heavy sleep. 

This condition is accompanied by great muscular relaxation; the head falls forward and the body loses all its strength and rigidity; if left to himself the subject will often fall from the chair and sink on the floor in a huddled mass. It is said by some that these subjects are insensible to suggestion; but, in a considerable number of cases of this description, it is possible to obtain a certain degree of susceptibility by adopting means calculated to strongly impress the subject with the suggestion; when this is gained, however, the response is often not decisive, and a great languor still characterizes the movements. 

The extent to which it is possible to obtain muscular contraction is clearly seen in the catalepsy of the entire body, when the whole of the voluntary muscles become absolutely rigid. In this condition, the body may be placed with the head on one chair and the feet on another, and will remain in a perfectly straight line between these two points. So complete is the rigidity that the body in this position will sustain an enormous weight without bending. A youth, by no means athletic or exceptionally strong, in this rigid state, can sustain, without any apparent uneasiness and without any harmful

result, a weight exceeding one hundred and fifty pounds. 

Nearly every one is familiar, if only by repute, with the ordinary phenomena of the hypnotic state; motor impulses can be inhibited; deafness, dumbness, blindness, in many cases, can be caused, the special senses can be suppressed, so that the strongest ammonia can be held under the nostrils without the patient evincing any sign of discomfort. The proper suggestions being made, he will eat pepper and think it is sugar, or eat a tablet of soap with gusto, under the impression that it is a piece of cake. Indeed, almost every sense and organic function of the body may be affected. Anæsthesia is present in the deeper stages, and can be increased by suggestion. 

The study of practical psychology has received but little help from materialists on account of the general neglect of its phenomena; the importance of a careful observation of the phenomena of hypnosis with regard to the psychological point of view can hardly be exaggerated. It is a field of work where the opportunities for definite experimental work are many; the condition should have been more employed than it has been up to the present; too many writers on psychology, however, appear to think that a casual acquaintance with the subject is all that is necessary as a basis of opinion. The psychical phenomena present are amongst the most striking characters of hypnosis and hypnotic suggestion. We have already referred to the lack of psychical activity present in the subject, when simply in hypnosis, without the effects of suggestion. The psychical action, however, is in some always limited; the powers of ideation are strictly limited to the quality of the suggestion; the subject does not wander off into other matters; he does

no voluntary thinking; the ideation present is in strict relationship to the stimulus supplied. If it be suggested that he is a General, he will act according to his conception of a General; he will give orders, treat those around him as soldiers and officers-and do many other things; but he will not act as his consciousness would generally tempt him to under ordinary circumstances; he will not give any sign of independent ideation; the relative merits of various Generals, the strategy of one, the knowledge of another, would not be described by him, even if in the normal condition he knew about these things, unless specific suggestion to this effect were given. The knowledge of a subject will undoubtedly materially affect the phenomena; thus a suggestion that he is fishing for trout to a boy who has never handled a rod and to an expert angler, would give very different results; but in both cases the concentration of the consciousness around the suggestion would still be the same; independent ideation would in both cases be equally conspicuous by its absence. From physiological considerations this limitation is important. 

Without decided suggestions it does not appear that the capacity of retaining ideas in hypnosis is materially increased. The writer has not been able to find any abnormal phenomena, nor have other arrived at a different result. The few experiments which they have made coincide with the above. When, however, it is suggested that there shall be an increase of memory, the results are most striking. On this point it is often very difficult to avoid unconscious suggestion, since the mere repetition of any words or lines frequently acts as a suggestion that the subject is to remember them.

.

Whilst, however, the retention of ideas for a long time is not increased, there is reason for thinking that, apart from suggestion, there is a greater capability for a short period, i. e., the subject, in hypnosis, would remember more of a speech five minutes after its delivery than he would in the normal state. 

To a subject quite ignorant of French, the writer repeated a French sentence, and found that he could recite more, after one repetition during hypnosis, than after one repetition in the waking state. The experiments on the point, though they all tend to confirm this view, have not been sufficiently extensive to justify the categorical assertion that this temporary increase is a common characteristic of hypnosis.

In the lighter stages of hypnosis the memory is perfect; in the hypnosis the subject's memory of his normal state is active, and, on the ending of the hypnosis, he is able to describe all that occurred. 

In deeper stages the memory is lost, unless, after awakening him, some hint be given to the subject; this may serve to bring the whole chain of events to his recollection. The writer will instance the case of his brother to whom, under hypnosis, he said, "Alles schweige jeder neige, ernsten Tonen nun sein Ohr." 

This sentence, his brother, on awakening, made many attempts to recollect, but entirely failed, until Heidenhain mentioned the word "schweige." The cases of this sudden recollection upon a hint being given are comparatively few in number, and in these cases, generally, the memory is hazy, and often resolves itself into some such question as, "Did you say anything about so-and-so?"

In the deepest states memory is entirely lost, the subject fails altogether to remember any event of the hypnotic sleep, and when anything he may have done under hypnosis is pointed out to him he manifests the greatest surprise.

A very important fact to be noticed is that, if the subject be hypnotized a second time, he will remember all the events of the previous hypnosis; and thus a deep hypnotic subject may be said to lead two distinct lives -the hypnotic, in which he remembers spontaneously all the suggestions which have been made and the events which have taken place during previous hypnoses, and the waking, in which he has no recollection or knowledge of these events. 

In a certain number of cases it would appear possible to obtain in hypnosis the recollection of events which the subject has long forgotten. This is, however, a difficult subject to investigate; we all fail to remember at times, and it is difficult to say whether or not in the normal state before the hypnosis the subject is exhibiting such a phase of merely temporary failure of recollection. Again, it is easy to induce a "recollection" in hypnosis simply as a result of the suggestion; the subject will give a full account of anything if only the suggestion be made that he is able to remember; but the "recollection" has no other basis. In consequence of these difficulties, experimental inquiry has not given any striking evidence. On the other hand, the induction of an altered condition, such as hypnosis, may be the means of spontaneously unlocking memory which has long ago ceased to affect the consciousness of the individual. For memory is largely a senso-motor process,

and the inhibition which is removed from certain neuronic groups would allow of an action impossible under normal conditions. The acute memory thus evolved is seen in the following case: 

A girl in a state of deep hypnosis was in a room at the Salpetriere, when Parrot, the physician to the refuge for Enfants assistes, entered. The subject was asked what was the stranger's name, and she replied, to the surprise of all present, and without hesitation, "M. 

Parrot." On awaking she declared that she did not know him; but after looking at him for a long while, she finally said, "I think that he is a physician at the Enfants assistes." When about two years old she had been for some time in this refuge, and had long forgotten the physician, whom she recognized with difficulty, on waking, while she could, in hypnosis, give his name when ordered to do so.

Another case, even more remarkable, is that of a woman of fifty, who was hypnotized, and who, to the hypnotist's great surprise, began to speak English, although no one present knew that she could understand a word of the language. During her waking state she did not know a single word of English, and it could be proved that, at any rate for twenty-five years, she had never spoken it. The only explanation possible is that, when very young, she must have had some knowledge of the language.

The hypnotic memory is an element which the experimenter has to carefully guard against, lest he be led into many errors by neglecting the necessary precautions. If, for instance, the operator has been in the habit of connecting some particular touch or pass with

the verbal suggestion of an act, he will find that, owing to the memory of the subject connecting the touch or pass with the verbal suggestion, he can dispense with speech, and rely solely on the touch for the production of the desired effect. This explains many of the tricks of the magnetizers, who have learnt, in the same way, to omit verbal suggestion, and thus add to the mystery of their performances.

One frequently finds the subject interpreting some act of the hypnotist as a suggestion; on a first hypnosis the writer slightly raised the subject's hand, and then by suggestion made it rigid ; in all hypnoses since it has been only necessary to elevate this hand to obtain the rigidity. The author told a patient to raise the left leg; as he gave the command he involuntarily took hold of the patient's right hand. When, in a later hypnosis, he took hold of the right hand, the patient immediately raised the left leg.

Another instance of the hypnotic memory often occurs during hallucinations. Thus, if a subject imagine himself a General, he will lead the regiment into the field, incite his men to press forward, and will hear all the noise incident to a battle; if, after several other hallucinations, he finds himself a child, some loud and unexpected noise during this imaginary childhood may serve to bring back the previous suggestion that he was a General, and he will promptly go through all his previous actions peculiar to this hallucination.
